LADIES' LErfEll.

MY DEAR NELLTE, I am going to begin this letter again about hats and toques, they being the principle thinga just now. Perhaps you will have seen the new style which has just. come out, which is a lovely black picture hat, with the top of the crown and the inner edge of the brim formed of gathered +macs of chiffon, and round the crown and forming the outer edge of the brim there is black sequin net, .thickly spangled. The brim is lined with tucked white tullw, and the hat is trimmed with black ostrich feathers and wings of net edged with black sequins. The particular charm of this hat lies in the graceful and becoming curves of the brim. Another one is a charming hat of "sun-kissed" straw, with chour of periwinkle chiffon and black plumes, which could be copied in many other colourings, or carried out entirely in black and white or black and Tuscan. That coat costume with velvet bands and small gilt buttons admits of variety in vests, and is especially effective in dove or mastic tints,, with darker velvet. The princesse model on the right is carried out in two colours, and simulates a separate skirt and coal

'AV.)

y^,u nbl.leetl the neat and fancy walking cos- tudies and jackets that are being made a lot of now It cannot be said that they are Hovel, yet they are so much and so well worn as to be quite a feature of the present season. The skirt, for in- stance, is of the latest medium out, full enough to be elegant, yet not bulky from excess of fulness. The basque of the coat has the points in front, from which it curves upwards and the small sleeves that the latest fashion dictates, and there is a semi-military smartness about the roll of the velvet facings. Some have a skirt of blue and white check cloth made up diagonally, and the coat is plain blue in the glossy new Paris cloth, with velvet of a darker tint. The large buttons are silver, and there are four of them, and the jabot is cream lace, and must be good, or it looks out of keeping with the fine cloth. The sleeve has no cuff, though the lower part is separated from the upper by two small velvet-covered cords, which cannot be put on neatly and must be inserted by cutting the cloth.

•1

The grey coats that are so very much worn with black cloth or silk skirts are decidedly "jaunty," and yet more severe than the cut-away coats that have recently been in favour. They have the great advantage of requiring no blouse or front under- neath them, and when the spring winds blow boisterously they give them no opportunity of penetrating, and always look trim' and neat One is a pretty thing of grey Amazon cloth with white silk machine stitching dovre the front, along the waist line, round the basque, and on the sleeves. The revers are covered with white moire and braided with black and white silk cord. The silver buttons are in groups of three, and on one side of the front only. A small coloured vest and neckband must be worn with this coat, so as to contrast with the white moire, and nothing looks better than blue. •

I mentioned, I believe, last week, some fancy girls' dresses. I should say new frocks are im- perative in spring for girls, who generally grow a good deal during the months that elapse between being fitted out for winter, and the bright though not always warm days' when everything feminine longs for fresh apparel. Mothers have little or no difficulty with the little ones, but the schoolgirls of from ten or twelve to sixteen have to be carefully considered. It is desimble to give them nice and pretty frocks to foster their own self-respect, and implant or encourage a love of what is fitting to their youth and position, but on the other hand no one wishes them to think overmuch about dress. A great many remain rather short and small till they have entered their teens, and in those cases there is no outdoor garment so useful as a pelisse, or entire dress put over the indoor one. A very nice one I have seen since writing is made of blue cloth with a closely-tucked blue silk yoke and neokbank and a frill round the neck of the same. The shaped collar or revers and the cuffs are of cloth embroidered with blue or cream silk, and finished with blue silk frills, and just where the plain skirt joins the short-waisted bodice there is a large chased silver or enamel button. A white felt hat with the narrowest possible binding of blue velvet, a long white ostrich feather on one side, and a stand-up quilling of white silk, edged with blue bebe velvet, looks extremely well with this costume. _

Your loving JffiNMl.

THE EXAMINER FRIDAY, MAY 5i 1899.

"WOMEN'S CHAT."

The first State hall of the season is to take place at Buckingham Vala.ce on the 18th inst., and the first State concert on June 2nd. About 1,600 guests have been in- vitf dby the Lord Chamberlain to the former function, everybody to appear in full dress. The invitations are for ten o'clock, but it is always Toast eleven before the Royal Procession enters the ballroom, conducted by the Great Officers of the Household. Dancing then begins, and goes on until the time for supper arrives, when a procepsirn is again formed, Royalty passing down tbe room between two rows of curtsying guests. In the supper room, the scene is very brilliant. for at the Royal table the gold plate is in use, and on the buffet the massive collecti n of dishes and tank irds is lavishly displayed. The loving cup is always there, with its lows of pegs at short distances apart, all down the inside of the cup. The menu is very good at the Palace suppers, the hock cup being the favourate beverage. _ _

The engagement of the young Queen of Holland is at yet officially announced, but it is believed that she is betrothed to 11:r cousin, Prince William of Weld, and that b 0 soon as the exact status and precedenca to be given the Prince, is decided upon, the offic at announce- ment will be made by the Dutch authorities. The Princely family of Wied is one of the oldest and richest in Northern Germany. Their principal residence is the Schloss at Neuwied on the Rhine, but the Princ,sa (Prince William's mother) who is a daughter of Prince Frederick of Netherlands, has a valuable property in Holland, besides a fortune of fifty millions of mailed. Dutch girls, like British ones, enjoy a good deal of freedom. In many ways indeed, they are allowed even more liberty than their British sisters. Unlike the French jeunes filles, who are never permitted to walk in the streets without a chaperon, who may never sr net to a young man without the permission of the'remott er the Dutch girls go about nearly everywhere alone. 'll ey walk alone, they do their shopping alone, they pay their calls alone, and are even allowed to go to dinner partle4 alone, without a chaperon. The dowry is almost an unknown thing in Holland, except among the very wealthy classes. Its absence however does not prevent the girls from finding husbands though it must be largely responsible for the long en- gagements which are the rule. These often last four or five years. In connection with them a curious custom may be noted. As soon as a girl becomes engaged, she is expected to take her fiance to pay a series of formal calls, in order that he may be ceremoniously introduced, not only to all her relatives, but also to all her friends. When this task is accomplished, she is free to go un- chaperoned with him wherever she pleasee. There is not much talk in Holland about " Women, Rights," yet Dutch women have many rights. The universities are open to them though very few avail themselves of this particular advantage. Lady Ulrica Duncombe's engagement is to be a short one, and before the summer is ended she will have taken up her residence in Amen Court. Lady Chloe resembles Lady Cynthia Graham perhaps more than her other sister, Lady Helen Vincent. But all possess the same beautiful peach-like complexions, which are probably due to the direct supervision of their mother Lady Feversharra. When they were small, she insisted upon thick bltre veils being donned over huge hats' even for the briefest run in the Srounos. The late Duchess of Leinister—the eldest of this lovely quartette—it will be remembered died of consumption some five or six years age. Lady Cynthia Graham, who is probably the most universally popular of all the sisters, spends compara- tively little of her time in London now, the best part of the year being passed at Netherley. Lady Helen Vincent on the other hand, is always to the fore during the en- tire London season, and later, flits about with the gay crowd, here, there, and everywhere. Lady Helen is considered by many, to be tee most beautiful woman of the day. Fair, tall, slender, with a rosebud mouth, and very musical voice. She was one of the most benutiful brides ever seen, and few who were pre- sent at her marriage will ever forget the picture she made in her exqaisite white gown brocaded in lilies. Bat for the plucky behavour of a young girl, yet another accident would have occurred at a wedding the other day, through the objectionable practice of rice- throwing. Some of the rice intended for the happy pair struck one of the horses which promptly bolted. A girl rushed forward, and dragged away the children who were standing in the road, at the same time seizing the runaway steed by the bridle which she managed to bold until help arrived. When rice at such functions is dis- pensed with altoeether, it will indeed be a haply day for all concerned. Rice spoils one's clothes, is dangerous to one's sight, and one's well-being generally, and ruinous to one's temper. At the Primrose wedding, silver con- fetti was employed, and the bappy pair given a "silver bath " as they left the house for the carriage, but prettier still is the petal shower, which happily grows more and more the vogue. A basket of flower petals is given to each guest, and the bride and bridegroom are pelted with these when leaving for the honeymoon. Last year it was possible to hold one's skirt up with a thumb and finger, this year it is two handfuls, and moreover it gives one the cramp to keep it out of the mud and dust. One never knows too, whether one is showing too ranch or too little petticoat. A method I have frequently seen adopted lately, and in many in- stances successfully, is to sew a couple of large flat but- tens inside the skirt. These cae be easily felt from the outside, and to catch the two buttons is the work of a second, when the dress is held gracefully. As to the ex- act position of the two buttons, this must be studied carefully, before a mirror. Little black silk jackets are in great favor at the moment, worn with black silk gowns, or those built of fabric of other description, and cider. Tho amateur home dressmaker should not attempt this particular little garment. A cape can easily be managed with the aid of a good pattern, but a black silk coat needs the greatest skill, otherwise " home made "is writ large all over it. When correctly turned out, it is a delightful covering. an I will probably he well worn until the bright summer days make their appearance. Shoes for walking may be of brown in all shades, from deepest chestnut, to light browns, and in all leathers, morocco preferably. These brown shoes should have brown buckles, but may if preferred be adorned with black ores. Brown shoes are the most serviceable we can indulge in, and are always smarter than the ordin- ary blacking shoe. Brown shoes which have lost the appearance of youth can have much of it restored to them by being laced up on trees, and thoroughly washed with soap and water and a nail brush- When absolutely clean they must be left on the trees a day or two to dry in the sere if this is available, and then carefully repolished. This treat- ment, besides removing all the dirt with which the polish has become impregnated, will also stretch out the wrinkles. It must be mentioned that the trees are apt to lose polish in the process,but this is a minor neitter. One of the finest things we can do for the complexion is to icdutge in a warm bath at night, and a C.icl, or if desirable,tepid one in the morning. Outdoor exercise of some description should be taken every day too. la's, hours rigidly avoided, alcohol dispensed with, and fruit and vegetables partaken of freely. Spinach is a capital vegetable for the comphxien. It is now becoming cheap, and is therefore within the reach of all. For the woman whose face is too thin, one of the finest things is a glass of hot milk every night, before retiring. It should he sipped slowly, ard a must of dry bread eaten to it. In a ehort time the long drawn look will disappear,and the cheeks fill out,giving their owner a youthful girlish appearance.

A MODERN ROMANCE.

Pk JOHN' BLOUNDELLE-BURTON, Author of "Fortune's my FOO, " "The Scourge of God," "Across the Salt Seas," "The Clash of Arms," "In the Day of Adversity," "A Gen- tleman Adven! Ater," "His Own Enemy," "Denounced," t,:c. •

SYNOPSIS OF PRECEDING CHAPTERS: Arabella Waldron, a young girl, is to sail for India in a ship belonging to her uncle, Captain Poole:7-, in order to be married at Bombay. But, previously, the reader is to he told of her courtship. A year before. the Captain had taken her to the theatre, where she had met Stephen Charlie. a young friend of her uncle, who is smitten by her. Afterwards, at a lunch a Portsmouth, Bella meets Gilbert Bampfyld, a flag-lieutenant to the Rear-Admiral, and at a ball in the evening she gives him most of her dances. After starting for Bombay to marry Gilbert, who is stationed on a warship there, a table comes for Bella not to start, as the warship has been ordered to Africa. She is, however, already out of reach.

CHAPTER VII. "AN OCEAN WAIF."

From the time the men had sheeted home the topsails as the Emperor of the Moon got • under way until now when, having left the Cape of Good Hope behind her, she was travelling through the water at a tremendous speed, her head set due north, scarcely anything had occurred much worthy of note. Soon—after the first two days had passed, during which time Bella had lain flat in her berth in the large roomy cabin provided for her by her uncle, the captain, while his wife had administered to her odd glasses of champagna, little cups of rich, succulent soup, and such like delicacies—the girl was able to reach the deck. And, once there and under an awning stretched from the ensign staff to past the mizzen mast, she would sit and meditate for hours on the forthcoming meeting with her lover, which drew nearer and nearer with each plunge of the Emperor's forefoot into the sapphire sea. Sometimes, too, she would read aloud a novel to Mrs. Pooley, who, perhaps because was good and motherly, and fat, would listen to she nothing but the most romantic love-stories which the vivid brains of fashionable lady novelists could turn out; though when alone and reading for her own amusement Bella would pore over books of adventure in wild parts of the world or devour some of the histories of marine adventure which her uncle possessed in his neat mahogany bookcases below.

Of Stephen Charke she saw, of course, a good deal, as it was natural she should do. You cannot be in a ship, however large or • small, without seeing much of all on board; but when it comes to sitting down every twenty-four hours to what Captain Pooley called "four square meals a day, with intervals between for refreshfnents," you must not only be brought into constant touch with your companions, but also enter into much discourse with them. Yet, as the girl told herself, Charke was behaving well, extremely well, so that, gradually, she lost all sense of discomfort that would other- wise have arisen through being thrown continuously into his society, and ere long she would observe his approach without the slightest tremor of suscepti- bility. Soon, too, she began to acknowledge to herself that Stephen was a gentleman in his feelings, and that, no matter what his sentiments might be towards her—if they existed still and were unchanged from what they had once been—he at least knew how to exercise that control over them which a gentleman should be capable of. Up to now, he had never said one word to her that any other person might not have uttered who had .found himself thrown into her society on board the Emperor of the Moon, nor had he unduly sought her presence or, being in it, endeavoured to remain there as long as it was possible. Nothing of much note had occurred thus far on the voyage, it has been said; yet there had of course been some of those incidents without which no voyage of any distance is ever made. Once through the Bay, and as they ran swiftly south, they had found themselves in a dense fog—a most unusual thing in such a latitude and at this time of the year; then upon the top of that fog 'there had sprung up a stiff breeze which gradually *developed into a gale, so that, from dewing the main royal to furling the top-gallant sails of the mizzen and foremasts was but a moment, as was . the next action of taking in the main top-gallant sail. And thus, ere long, Bella had her first experience of what a storm at sea was like, and, as she heard the live stock grunting and squealing forward; the ship's furniture more or less thunder- ing about wherever it could get loose; the piano— on which, only the night before, she had played the accompaniment to her uncle's deep bass voice as he trolled out "In Cellar cool "—thumping heavily against the bulkhead•to which it was usually lashed, and the cries of the sailors as they uttered 'words which might not perhaps be properly denominated as "cries" alone, she began to wonder how her darling Gilbert could ever have chosen such a calling. Also, the streaming planks when, at last, she ventured on deck, the dull sei i clouds and the mournful look of the Emperor herself, under reefed topsails, foresail, fore topmast staysail, fore- sail, main trysail and spanker, as she rolled and lolloped about in the troughs and hollows of the sea, and took the water first over one bow and then over another—and then, for a change, over her stern--only increased that wonder. _

Yet, lof the next morning—for the sea is a great quick-change artist, volatile and variable as a flirt, though too often as tragic as Medea herself— when Bella looked out of her scuttle, against which the green water now slapped boisterously but not viciously—all was changed. A bright sun shone down from the heavens, the ship had still got a roll on her, though not an unpleasant one—and the girl felt hungry. Which was as good a sign that the storm was over as could well be wished for. _ _

"It has been a rough night," Stephen Charke said, as he rose from his breakfast on her entrance to the saloon, helped her to her chair, and bade the steward bring coffee and hot rolls and bacon— all of which were already perfuming the air. "Your uncle is now on deck. We have been there all night." "I thought," said Bella, pouring out her coffee and smiling pleasantly, since now all fear had departed from her mind that Charke would mis- construe any friendly marks of intimacy she might be disposed to graciously bestow on him, "that I was at the end of my journey. Indeed, that we all were; that it was brought to a sudden end; accomplished. That—" "That," said Stephen, smiling too, sadly enough, yet enchancing wonderfully his dark, handsome looks by doing so, "that perhaps Mr. Bampfyld might miss his bride!" _ _

For a moment Bella's hazel eyes flashed at Him, and he thought how wondrously beautiful they looked as they did so—then a serious expression came on her face. While, after pausing a moment, as though scarcely knowing what answer to make or whether she should make one at all, she said: "Yes. That he might miss his bride. My death, and that alone, could cause him to do so." And as she spoke she looked Stephen straight in the face, feeling again—which she regretted—that old sentiment of doubt of him which she had come to believe she had conquered and subdued. "His death would cause the same result," he answered, speak'ng slowly, hesitatingly, for in truth he felt as if he were treading on dangerous ground. In a moment he found such was the case. "Mr. ' Charke," the girl said, very quietly now, "I should be so much obliged to you if, during the remainder of our journey together, you will neither discuss my affairs nor those of my futun husband, nor him. It will make the voyage pleasanter to me if you will do that." As she spoke the bell struck two, and, since the watches had been disorganised by the storm of the night, that sound meant that Captain Pooley would row come below for his breakfast, and his place bovo be taken by the mate. Therefore, he turned low ids the stairs, muttering: "I beg your pardon, I am sure. Pra ; forgive me. I will not offend again." Then he disappeared on to the deck. Yet an hour lister he stood by her side beneath the 'awning, and now he was directing her attention to something that, a mile off, was the object of the attention of everyone on board. The captain and his wife were both regarding it fixedly; so, too, were the men forward; the only persons not present being, of course, the watch below and the second mate, Mr. Fagg, who had now turned in. "What do you make it out to:be ? " asked Pooley of Stephen, as they still gazed at it. "It is not a baby nor a child; yet it is scarcely bigger than the first. Can it be a dog ? " "No " said Charke authoritatively, as though hi, yo nger eyesight was not to be disputed; "it is eit r a young tiger or a !panther cub afloat on a water-cask. There has been a !wreck during the

night, I expect, and it ilaB got adrift. I-wimps," he said, "if we cruise around a bit we may find some human life to save."

- "How should it be aboard any ship ? " asked the captain. "Who takes tigers or panthers for pas- sengers?" "Plenty of people," Charke answered quietly. "They are brought home to sell to the menageries and 'zoos. A cub like that is worth twenty pounds —worth looking after. Ganes bring them home sometimes, sailors often. • Meanwhile," he added, "according to the set of the waves, that thing will be alongside us in a quarter of an hour. I'll bet a day's pay it strikes the ship betwixt the main and mizzen channels."

"Oh!" exclaimed Mrs. Pooley and Bella together, "do let us save it and get it on board! It will," said the latter, "be such a lov.Ay plaything—and such a curiosity! Fancy a girl from West Ken- sington who has never had a plaything or pet mora stupendous than a canary, a cat, or a fox-terrier, having a tiger. Why!" she exclaimed, with a laugh which gave to her short upper lip an appear- ance of tantalising beauty, "Una will be outdone by me—a girl of the nineteenth century!" Tantalising or not as that smile might be, it led to the salvation of the cub, for, with a swift look at the captain which was meant to ask for his assent, Charke called to one of the sailors to get over into the channels and down on to the fourth futtock, he telling him with wonderful accuracy the exact spot where the water-cask would strike the stationary ship. Five minutes later, that which he had calculated with such precision came to pass, the cask touched the vessel's side almost immediately beneath the man's feet and, in another moment, the cub had been caught by its loose skin in the exact middle of its back and hauled up, squealing, spitting and scratching, on to the deck. "The little beast! " exclaimed the mate, as he sucked the back of his hand where the creature had clawed him; "the little beast! this is a pretty reward for our saving it from drowning!" and then he administered a sound kick to the thing as it lay on the deck. So sound and rousing 'a one, indeed, that it emitted a grunt of pain, and with its claws —about as big, or bigger, than those of a good- sized cat—endeavoured to fasten on to his legs. Also, from its yellow, scintillating eyes, it emitted a glance of such malignant ferocity as, had it been more fully grown, might have alarmed a braver man than he.

"Oh! how cruel to kick that poor little half- drowned thing!" Bella exclaimed reproachfully; "it never meant to hurt you, only it was frightened. Poor little thing!" she said again, and, even as she spoke, she knelt down on the deck and stroked the wet striped ball that lay there. And it seemed as if her gentleness had some power to soothe what- ever ferocious instincts still dormant and undeveloped at present—were nevertheless within it. For, instead of now using its paws as weapons with which to strike out and attack anything near it, it played with her as a kitten plays with a ball, tapping at her hand and trying to catch it, and pushing and kicking against her with its hind legs.

"Yoii see," she s-aid, looking up at Charke with a glance in which she could not disguise her dislike of his violence; "you see at present, at least, it does not try to harm those who treat it well." _ _ _ .

"Yes," lie said, "I see." Then he added, half bitterly, half morosely, "No one doubts your powers of fascination, Miss Waldron."

CHAPTER VIII. "HIS NAME IS - WIIAT ? "

The saving of this creature which Bella elected to call Bengalee, because she said she as sure it cane from Bengal, and also because she had once sung a song of that name, was followed by•no other events of any imnortance whatever. Nor need their stay at the Cape be dwelt upon, because it consisted simply of various visits which were paid in the outskirts by Mr. and Mrs. Pooley, accompanied by Bella; by the unloading of a considerable portion of the cargo of The Emperor of the Moon, and by the refilling of the hold with other goods sale- able in India.

And now they were once more on their way towards the Equator, going due north instead of due south, as when they had last approached it, and with a cool southern breeze driving the Emperor along under full sail. Yet, so gentle was this breeze, that even if there had been any who were not sailors in the ship—which Bella, as well as Mrs. Pooley, might now be well considered to be, after the length of voyage she had already gone through, added to a few extra days and nights of turbulence and storm—scarcely would they have felt any inconvenience from the motion. Thus, therefore, with occasionally a dropping of the wind which reduced their speed a few knots, and, some- times, with a total drop in it, so that they did not progress a knot an hour, while the ship swung slowly round and round the compass, they found themselves at the time which is about to be de- scribed in about Latitude 2010 S., and Longitude 60'50 E., or, as near as may be, about two hundred and fifty miles to the east of Mauritius. Wherefore, since the Emperor of the Moon has arrived thus far in the Indian Ocean there has to be set down a series of strange events which befel her, of so remarkable and peculiar a nature that one wonders that those events have never been chronicled before. For, far different from the ordinary stress and disasters which overtake ships at sea were those which have to be described; far different from those which the recorders of maritime calamities are in the habit of chronicling either in romance or dryasdust descriptions of facts.

All of which the writer now proceeds to relate, beginning with a strange coincidence that, perhaps, none but those readers who, in their voyage through life have recognised that truth is more strange than the wildest fiction, will, perhaps, be willing to allow as within the bounds of likeli- hood. However, to make a beginning, since the coincidence is true, the Emperor of the Moon was in those latitudes above described when, it being a bright hot morning with the sea already gleam- ing like molten brass, with the pitch between the planks already of the consistency of putty, and with the brasswork in such a state of heat that it was unsafe to touch it unless one wanted to leave the skin of their palms and fingers behind them, the look-out on the fo'castle head yelled : "Sail, right ahead! " Now, since nothing of this hind, neither steamer nor sailing vessel had been seen since they had northed out of the west-wind drift, and since, also, the liners were rarely found out- side the Equatorial current, this cry was sufficient to fill every one on board with a considerable amount of interest and excitement.

" Whereaway ? " called out Charke, who was on the poop at this moment, the captain, his wife and Bella being below at breakfast, and ere the man could repeat that the sail was right ahead and about five miles off, all those others there had come on deck.

"How pretty it looks, shining in the sun!" the girl exclaimed, as she regarded it through a pair of marine glasses which her uncle had placed at her disposal, "and how the sail glistens. It looks like a star."

"Humph!" said the captain, as be gazed through his binocular. "Like a star! True enough, so it does. And," he said, addressing the two mates who were standing near him, "we have seen such stars hereabouts before, eh? Do you think," he went on; addressing Charke, lowering his voice a little, "it is one of those? " "Don't know," Charke said, working his own glass a good deal. "Can't see how it can be; too far to the Fast. Bussorah, Muscat, Ras-el-Hadd, Mohamralt, Oman that' s their mark. What should they be doing here?" "All the same," exclaimed Pooley, "it's the rig, and the true shape, that of a Jargonelle pear cut in half. Ido believe that's what it is. They might have been blown out of their, course, you know, or chased by one of her Majesty's ships. What do you think?" "I think," said Charke, who always spoke of everything connected with his calling in the most unemotional manner possible, "I think we shall know when we come up to her, as we must do in about half an hour. Also," he continued, with a subdued tone in his voice, while his eye glinted sideways towards where Bella stood, "we are not naval officers but only humble merchant seamen. There's no prize-money for us, therefore it is not our business. _

Bella had, of course, been listening attentively to all that had been said since she had come on deck, and ran lightly up the poop ladder, and now, hearing these words about "naval officers" and "prize-money," her interest became more intense than before.

"Oh uncle!" she exclaimed, putting her hand on his sleeve, "what does it all mean? Naval officers and prize-money! That's not one of her Majesty's ships, surely? my dear," the captain replied, "Wt.. 's not ond of her Majesty's ships; but I shall be precious surprised if she doesn't turn out to be one of the ,very craft that her Majesty's ships are always on the look-out for hereaLouts, only rather closer in towards the African coast than this. She has all the build of a Arab slave dhow." "Ave." exclaimed Charlie. who was still using

his glass Melly, trcm where he stood behind therfi. "Aye, and something more than the build, too. If I'm not mistaken her hatches have open gratings. What do you say, Fagg ? " turning to his junior. "Seems so, sir," said that young officer, who, never wasted more words than necessary, "though I'm not quite sure." "I am," replied Charke. I can see the grating slits perfectly as we get nearer." "What does that mean ? " asked Bella, to whom this conversation conveyed nothing. "It means," said Pooley, "that there is live stock below those gratings. Black cattle, as they used to' be called on the West Coast. Ordinary hatches, to simply cover up cargo, are not made to let the air in. That can do without breathing." "How awful!" Bella exclaimed, while through her mind there ran recollections of what she had heard or read casually of the slave trade in the old days, and also of the horrors of the North-West passage. "How awful!" "Bad enough," replied Pooley, "though not as bad as the old West African days, nor as you might think. The slave trade is a valuable one in this ocean, and those who are carried in the dhows are well enough fed. Rice, Indian corn, maize, and cassara, is given them for food, and they have mats and matting galore to sleep on. Persian merchants and Arab gentlemen don't buy starved scarecrows for their domestic servants.' "Whatever she is, and whatever her cargo is, there's something wrong with her," the chief mate suddenly exclaimed. "She's off the wind now, and the fellow' who was at the helm has left it. It is abandoned. By Jove!" he exclaimed, "he is lying wriggling by it. What on earth's the ma' t cr ? " "And," aided the second mate, "there's a negro woman waving a red scarf. Something's wrong there, no doubt." "We shall be up to them in ten minutes," the master said, all bustle and excitement now, "let go the foretack. Stand by to lower the starboard- quarter boat," and, as he spoke, the men of the wat-li who had been leaning on the fife rail rushed to the falls to be ready to let the boat sink to the watcr when the proper moment came. To Bella • this seemel the most exciting moment of her life! There, in front of her, was one of those vessels, the name of which, or class of which, was almost unknown to her, excent that, from odds and ends of conversation with her lover, she had gathered that these were the things in the chasing of which part of his existence might be passed until they met once more at Bombay. Here, on this glistening, glassy sea the dhow lay—her one mast raking towards her bow instead of her stern, as is the case with most vessels of the Western world, her long white triangle of a sail unfilled and flapping listlessly. A dhow—perhaps a slaver! as her uncle and the master had said—a dhow in sore distress with, writhing by her helm, the man who had lately been steering her, and, over her bow, that negro woman waving frantically the red scarf. .Excite- ment there was, indeed, in all this, causing her, even as she eagerly watched the vessel they were approaching, to wonder how the striking up of the band at a ball, or the ring of the prompter's bell ere the curtain rose on a drama that all London was flocking to see, could have ever stirred her' pulses. What were they, those trivialities, to the smiling glistening face of this Eastern sea—to the horror and the cruelties that this ever tranquil ocean's 'bosom had enfolded through the ages. Still the negress waved, not recognising, perhaps, in her blind, besotted, dumb, animal-like ignorance that help was at hand, and still, through their glasses, they could see that he who had steered the dhow now lay motionless. Then, at her ear, as the Emperor came within six cables lengths of the dhow, her uncle gave a few rapid orders; the second mate, accompanied by the boat's crew, jumped into the quarter-boat—the man at the wheel luffed until the vessel had not a motion in her. Swiftly the boat was lowered, the rudder and thole pins shipped, and she was on her way to the dhow. • "What do you make of it? "the master roared to Fagg ten minutes later, as, by then, the Emperor- of the Moon had come closer to the dhow through the motion of the swell. "What? " " I don't know what to make of it, sir," the second mate called back. "The man who was steering is, I think, dead. He does not move, and there is a white film over his eyes. The woman , who waved the handkerchief seems well, but I cannot understand what she means. She does nothing but howl and point below." "Are the hatches grated ? " "Yes, sir, and there are four negroes beneath them. It is a slave dhow for certain. The negroea are shackled and handcuffed." "Have you searched further ? " "I am going to do so now. The ship is settling, I think. There is a kind of poop super-structure forming cabins. " "Search at once; then bring all alive on board us. -

In a moment Mr. Fagg had disappeared into what he had termed a "kind of poop super- structure," and while he was in it all on board the Emperor were occupied in speculating on what could have brought a slaver so far to the East and out of her 'ordinary course; and also in wondering what the mate would. find during his further search. - -

But that wonderment was soon to be resolved, for ere Mr. Fagg had been out of their sight five minutes, he rushed back from the super-structure to the deck, and bawled through his hands: "There is a young naval officer lying in the poop cabin, and he is slightly wounded. His name is- "

"What ? " roared rooley, astonished at the mate's hesitation.

--- -------_____ "It is marked on the rim of his cap—inside. It is—l----I am afraid it is Miss Waldron's fianei. The—the—name is Bampfyld."

BUSII LIFE IN ' A.WARALIA.

"There is no more delightful experiesce than a whole day's ride with tie eve s.!‘l-rinund a big Australia:4 run." writes Auton Bertram in the May number uf the "Wind- sor Magazine." " You the in the coil early morning and breakfast, before the tun is we 4 up, on beefsteak, fresh scones, quince jelly, and groat cups of tea. Mean- while the horses have been driven into the yard : you take your bridle, and, selecting your own steed cut of the thirty or forty there, you elge him hit() a corner and Eucre 'd in getting the bit into his mouth. Then you saddle him and ride off into the hush, thz- sun shining on the grey, quivering gam lea,es, or on the glistening foliage of the box. The overseer rides through each paddock in turn, noticing the condition of the sheep, in- specting the creek or water-welt, keeping art eye to the fences when he passrs them. At mid-day you draw up at a boundary rider's hut. He is married perhaps, and his wife ',pleads her cleanest cloth and g vs you lunch, followed by tea (always tea in the bush), while the overseer retails to the lady all the gossip of the station. Then on again. Perhaps you come out into the open plain. Four or fire miles ahead you disiiern the bright colours of a mob of cattle, and you canter towards them. You find a band of men galloping round a thnisaud cattle in a circle, cutting out the fat ones, or era rating the bul- locks firm the cows. You stay and j in them ; and when the wells is finh,hed, and the cattle are separated. counted, and draitcd off, you all rite off together to the next boundary rider's hut. This man is a bachelor, per- haps, and his hut is a plain struczure of rough wood, but his ' hilly 'is on the fire, arra is soon brought to a boiling pit. He limos you rr,u:id strong-brewed tea in tin panrukins, well sweetened with brown sugar, but without milk, nd therevith cuts vou chucks of fresh 'damper.' rhe men talk about the lith,ur party, the coming election, the betting odds, the ruxt great race- meeting at Melbourne, or the last ;:asn of sheep-3te3ling in the dhitrie,. And now the sun is nearing the edge of the great yellow plain. It is time to be off. You mcust once more, and all canter ho:ne tie/ether amid the rich heel of the Australian sun,ot. As the darkness falls you ride into the. station, well tired with your forty miles' ride, and quite ready for your dinner at the hospitable table of your hoist, and for the evening in the drawing-room Afterwards, where you sit listening to music, talking about the old country, or hearing tales of early days ha the now.'

WARRINGTON TOWN COUNCIL.

The Town Council met on Tuesday evening. the Deputy Mayor (Alderman Faircleugh) presiding. Then were also present :—Aldermen Roberts. &Mon, Hutchinson ; Councillors Shaw Green, Monks, Plump- ton, Bennet:, Pierpoint, Smethurst, Eleaton, Jolla Evans, James Evans, Longtort, Ellison, Burton, Tinniou, Rogers, Jolley, F. R. Roberts, Shillcock, Lee, J Roberts, with Mr. J. Lyon Whittle (two clerk), Mr. Stanley Wils3n (deputy towa clerk), Mr. Faireurst (borough surveyor). Mr. Dens (sanitary engineer). ABSENCE OF THE MAYOR. In the absence of the Mayor, Alderman Fairc'ough, deputy -mayor, was voted to the chair, on the motion of Alderman SUTTON, seconded be Councillor HEATON. MUSEUMS AND GYMNASIUMS ACT, 1891. A special meeting was held for the purpose of passing the following resolution ;—"That the Mayor, Al terrnen, and Burgesses of the Borough of Warrington, acting by the Council as the urban authority for the said borough, do hereby adopt the Museums and Gymnasiums Act, 1801, so far as it relates to museums only, and that this resolution shall come into operation on the sth day of June, 1899." Alderman ROBERTS moved the confirmation of the resolution, and pointed out that the Act woul I enable too Corporation to make bye-laws for the regulation of the management of the Museum, and impose a penalty for offences. They had no power at present to borrow mon, y for the purchase of land and buildings, but only suCh borrowing powers as were covered by the rate d for the purpose. Toe main provision was to pro- 'vide the opportunity to levy a special rate of one half- penny in the £. That rate would not be laid until a special resolution had been brought bifore 'he Council and sanctioned, and it was not likely it would be imposed at present. ;Hear, hear.) Councillor MONKS seconded. Councillor SHAW GREEN said that bad it not been for the explanation which Alderman Roberts had .made he might have had something to say about the matter. He accepted his promise on behalf of the committee that they would not seek to impose the halfpenny rata for some time to come. The motion was carried. INFECTIOUS DISEASE NOTIFICATION ACT, 1889. A special meeting of the Council was also held for the purpose of passing the following resolution .—" That the Mayor, Aldermen and Burgesses of the Borough of Warrington, acting by the Council as the local authority fer the said borough, do hereby adopt tree Infecti us Disease (Notification) Act, 1889, and that this resolution shall come into operation on tire fifth lay of June, 1899.' Alderman SUTTON moved the adoption of the re s du- tion, and drew attention to the fact that if the Ace were adopted its provi ions would superesde Sectiol 23 of the local Act of 1879, which wou d cease to be in operation. The Netification Act 1339 extended the de- finition of infectious disease t) membranous croup ad eryeioelaq, which were not included in their Lica! Act. Notification of these diseases would be necessary in ad- dition to the other diseases named in the Act. The same feea would be payable to the medical practitioners in cases occurring in private practice, but reduced the fee to is. in Cases occurring in the prftctice of a medical ! officer of any public body or institution. One advantage of the Act was that it applied to every ship, vessel,boat, telt, van, shed, or similar structures used for human habitation. The Act did not differ in any oteer respect from the provisions new in force. Alderman ROBERTS seconded, and the resolution was agreed to.

FINANCE AND ESTATE COMMITTEE. IRREDEEMABLE SECURITIES. The CHAIRMAN at a meting ef the above committee, read a report pespared by the deputation appointed to attend the conference in London, on the question of irredeemable securities. Councillor SHAW GREEN moved the confirmation of the minutes, and alluded to the question of the irredesmable securities. He said a good deal of important information was, conveyed at the conference to everyone who attended it, and some of the resolutions were come to with the hope eventually of dealing with these particular securities. They had some in Warrington called the gas annuities, and they could not compel the holders to sell. They had no power to demand such securities in exchange for any amount of money. They hoped that something would be done to convert these irredeemable securities into redeemable securitiee. The DEPUTY-MAYOR seconded, and the minutes were adopted. CARRIAGE SHEDS IN eRPLEY STREET. DOCTORS SAID THE CASE WAS INCURABLE.

Tux story recsntly told by Miss P. Shapcott. of No. 4, Kenwin-terrace, ElLacombe, Torquay, to a local re- porter, was a surprising one. Miss Shapcott is a dress- maker, and consequently, has few opportunities for out- door exercise. " About eleven years ago," she said, "I had my first illness, and the doctors told me I was suffering from gastric ulcer. I brought up a quantity at blood on several occasions ; then the horrible pain I suffered would diminish fo: a time. About three years since, the complaint returned in a more acute form. When the abscess broke, I hoped I should got wall, bot it gathered again and became terribly painful. The doctors told me my case was incurab'e, and I was advised to go into a hospital, but this I declinod to I do. The slightest portion of solid food caused me excruciating pain, aud lived on nothing but "slops.' My face became wan and thin, and remarks on the pain I suffered were general. One day when I was at borne in a very melancholy condition, a little bock was pushed under the door, and turning over the pages I came across a cas, exactly similar to my own, that was cured by Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People. I made up my mind at once to try them, and buying a I box, took the pills according to directions. I have taken , fourteen boxes, and now feel absolutely cured, which is astonishing when you remember that I suffered for eleven years. My friends regard it as really marvellous and Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People are held in high esteem by them. I wish you to publish this statement, my sole purpose being that all who suffer like myself, should know that there is still hope tor them."

At the tim3 of the aersement two sets were engaged on these terms,but afterwards there were more. This novel plan answered so well that the defendants were inundated with orders obtained ia this way. The Richmond Stove Company had a fire at their premises, and for a time their eutpat of s-,eves eras reatrieted,aed on this account they, in July. 1893, entered into a fresh agreement with the plaintiffs, ter which only four sets, consisting of three lady canvassers esce instead of four, were to be employed instewl of fire sets, but the period during which each se',' then engaged was to go on working for the Me eianti was ext lathed from a limit period ot 13 weeks, except in th 4 case of one set, to Decemeer 31, 1893, the charge for each set as befere, being £lB per week. THE EX MIN Ell,

THE CLIANOELLOR of the The Eclips, of Sir :a. EXCHEQUER had a sudden Hicks-Beach.

rise with the formation of the present Government. He seemed to be the - strong man of the group. He was the man with the least nonsense about him of any of them. When occasion called for strong speech he was 'ready with it, and when strong actions were• required he was equally to be relied upon. We remember how he seemed to lead when fears were - expressed about the Soudan advance involving us in anyßritish expenditure, and with what a revul- sion of feeling we had to pay down our hundreds of thousands. When he was eloquent about there being worse things than war he obtained applause. It seemed as if we could rely upon his patriotism, and that he was a resisting force against all absurdities and erratic courses. How does he stand now? As a prophet he is discredited, as a guardian of our finances against raiders he appears to be losing ground, and as an orthodox financier he suffers from the contagion of bad com. pany. He anticipated a surplus in order to meet the demand of men he seemed unable to resist, and he has now grabbed advantages accruing in. years to come in order to avoid asking the coun- try to contribute more money to payfor the policy of the Government n nv. These are acts cf weak- ness, and if they are to bo described as clever they savour of the cleverness known ir the City, and associated with co npanies whose recordis not of the orthodox kind. His financial expedients can- not be defended without ridiculing financial purism and lugging in extraneous matters. The fact stares us in the face that he has avoided asking the supporters of the Government to vote new taxes for the payrn 3nt of new burdens. This

electioneering, and not patriotic finance. The nevv taxes he has ventured to impose will disturb our relations with witn-growing countries. and with our wine-producing colonies, at a time, when we wish to do more trade with the one and enter upon closer and more friendly relations with the other. The small payers of income-tax will have to contribute the bulk of the n 3w light wine duties. The stamp duties on bonds will reduce our financial business with the colonies, and begin change in the centr ti financial position of Lon- don which may have serious consequences. wet aan but applaud him for resisting the temptation to obtain favour by guaranteeing a Rhodesian Railway; but the fact is injurious to. the repute- ion he had obtainel aalyagst his friends of an Tinperial Chancellor. He has proved himself to be so weak that we have lost confileric 3 in his sagac- ty and his strength. Surpluss make life easy for any Chancellor. It is when delis aaive that we find out of what mottle thy are m tde. Expendi- ure goes on gaily until novel policy begins to ing,.

Cre£l,E Chan affect tatin had ende

crease it, and when the bill comes in it is the Chancellor of the Exchequer whose reputation is affectei. Sir M. HICKS-BEACH has lost his repu- tation as a financier; and if the House of Commous had voted as it thinks, his Budget would have ended the Ministry.

SOME useful points in The Majority Licensin' the majority report of the Report. Licensing Commission inay be considered. The minority report is that With which the name of LORD PEEL is associated, and this is not referred to on the present occasion. It is admitted that drunkenness is still a gigantic evil, and that "hardly any sacrifice would be too great" which would tend to its diminution. To reduce the number of houses and to simplify the law are held to be important points. As to the law, some of the suggestions made would improve administration. It is recommended that the Licensing Court should be a body chosen annually by each petty sessional bench, and numbering not lase than three or more than seven. The appeal Court should be appointed by county and borough justices, and the number should be fixed by the Home Secretary. For county boroughs the justices should appoint a fixed num- ber, and the Recorder should be ex-officio chairman. No change in hours is reCOM- tended. Temporary suspension of licenses instead of fines is recommended in some cases, re- gistration instead of endorsement, and the regis- tration of all clubs. As to reduction of licenses, a scheme is suggested based on compensation "equi- valent to the fair intrinsic selling value of the license and goodwill." The money is to come from the trade itself by an annual tax of one-third per cent., off-licenses to be included, hotels and res- taurants to pay on a scale of one-sixteenth of their Tateable value, clubs to contribute, and the rent of new licenses, determinable by public tender, to be added. The scheme would work in septennial periods, and at the beginning of each period a sum could be borrowed to operate with, secured upon the tax. Every county or borough could thus as- certain how many licenses it could extinguish in seven years. We are very much afraid the num- ber would be very small. On the vexed el:lesbian of tied houses the majority report is not decisive. They do not think it leads to more drinking. Its good working depends on the brewer. The abolition of the system is regarded as impracticable, and that of dual licenses would not work well. Toe licensee should be, it is thought,the only person dealt with by the licensing authority, and in the event of misbe- haviour the house as well as the license should be affected. It is further held to be established that adulteration by retailers with deleterious ingre- dients is not demonstrated. The at:Ando of the majority towards local option or local control is decidedly negative, but it is said that "iii the event of municipalities being empowered to ac- quire licensed premises we see no reason for objecting to such a proposal, as far as the licens- ing laws are concerned." It is noted,however,that :if there were no compensation municipalities Would claim to take over the liquor traffic on such terms, and be impelled in that direction by a de- sire to make profits. This,say the majority, "would be contrary to public policy, as municipalisation ought to be adopted, if at all, on public grounds, and not for profit." But there must be profit,and the ethical dispute is as to how it shall be dis- tributed. The report, we fear, will hardly be re- garded as leading us any great distance, on the oath of reform.

The House of Commons will be strengthened this week by the addition to its membership of two distinguished university men in place of deceased members. Sir William Anson, who has resigned the Vice-Chancellorship at Oxford in order to take a seat at St. Stephen's, and Professor D. M. Edwards, who is a college Don, and follows Mr. T. Ellis, are both unused to the political arena,and n will be interesting to see to what extent they Can adapt themselves to their circumstances. It c_raonot be said that Professor Lecky, Professor 'ebb, or Mr. Talbot, who are most prominently associated with university representation now, are great forces in the debates in the House. They are lacking in the quick retort, in the anticipation of aygument,and especially in the sense of humour -which salts the meat of politics and is required to Make L acceptable. Readers, however, would greatly err if they thought that because these embers do not often speak, and when they do are lacking in effect, they are not useful to the Youse. There is much leavening of opinion done ln the inner rooms of the:best clubs in London, ard he influence of men like Lecky,Jebb, and Talbot !IS strong on the commercial minds and on the too It!tensely practical. Their educational work is not given up when tkey come to St. Stephen's though both they and their pupils are unconscious that it Is being continued.

That the personality of Mr. Cecil Rhodes has not -lost any of its spell was abundantly evident by the proceedings in the Cannon-street Hotel on 4Y-Londay. It was not the crowd that impressed— though shareholders waited hopelessly fromnine ?clock, when the hall filled, for a meeting timed to begin at noon—hut the enthusiasm with which ' a nenly financial body of men, assembling in an :tunoaphere of small profits and quick returns— ? r the City of London is, above all places, g!v.en to rapid monetary transactions—listened to 171.81.0ns of future greatness. There have been no utvidends on Chartereds at present, but that fact I,lnver effects the warmth of the welcome of Mr. hodes. No doubt there was satisfaction in his !tatement that the company had two millions in ;land ; but the shareholders cheered almost as dly the scheme to raise three millions for the 7.jaPe-to-051r0 Railway by the issue of the remain- shares in the Company at a premium price of as against the original par value of £l. The great skill of the oratory of Mr. Rti ides is in its 4Pparent self-restraint. When he tells you that he 9nuld open up vis Las of wealth, but he is afraid of Inflating the money market and encouraging 813.0culation, he leaves the mind under a greater 41raulus than if he had filled in a mass of Vails, rPo!"1 which he might have Deen criticised the fl- ing year if they did not all turn out right. Mr. Odes is an impressionist artist, who always 'alc.na his hearers grateful to him in the sense t2lat gratitude is a lively anticipation of things to come.

Lord Hawke, Mr. W. G. Grace, and Mr. H. W. 14" S ofteu said that figures can 1-)L-3 made to IP° anYthing, and while tha statement Is open

to doubt in some cases, there is certainly a sub- stratum of truth in it. If figures cannot" prove anything " they can be made to alter the aspect of things in a very material manner. Qiite recently the President of the London Institute of Civil Engineers showed that he appreciated the old axiom by attempting to convince his hearers that a seat in a first class train is the safest place in the world. In the same period, he says, in which 24 persons were killed on the railroad by accidents for which they were not responsible, 1,800 people were suffocated in bed, 148 were choked by food in dining-rooms, and 925 fatalities resulted from falling downstairs. In all probability the figures are quite true but they do not support the speaker's contention with proof. It is possible to arrive at such comforting statistics by selecting. the " period." In fact the President of the In- stitute might as well have clinched the matter by taking a "period" in which no railway fatalities occurred at all but in which a number of people made their exits from this world by accidents such as he mentions. Then again the fact that there are thousands of people takingthese risks to every one of those who imperil themselves on the rail- way seems to have been overlooked by him. The theory is very ingenious and the figures have a comforting look, but they will not stand analysis if they are to be advanced as proof that a seat in a railway carriage is the safest place in the world.

Mr. Henry Thornton was inclined to be critical at the Warrington Rural District Council meet- ng, and hadsomething to say against the election of bothchairman and vice-chairman. His objec- tions werenot very serious in either instance and did notaffect the voting. Mr. J. W. Knowles, be said,was not solid enough—there was two much wind about him. Now this is absolutely the last charge:we should have imagined anyone bringing against Mr. Knowles.lf solidity is a requisite in candidates for the vice-chaitmanship, then we shouldthink Mr. Knowles admirably fitted for the post. There is no spectral transparency about him; he looks solid enough to fill any chair. But Mr. Thornton is not satisfied. What amount of solidity he expects a man to possess he does not state, but he must be an unreasonable individual if Mr. Knowles does not come up to his expectations in this respect. Mr. Knowles was a little hurt at the reflection cast upon him—and what more natural than that he should be. If we may, we would suggest to Mr. Knowles that he sits upon I the next man who dares to question his sllidity. We can think of no more effectual manner of refut- ing the accusation, and moreover it would do some people a world of good to be sat on.

Evidence of the growing success of the Man- chester Ship Canal as a commercial enterprise is constantly being afforded. Occasionally there are some encouraging statements made at one of the periodical meetings of the Co:npany, and quite as often there are important developments of traffic to raise the spirits of the somewhat de- spondent shareholders. Much has been done to demonstrate the value and usefulness of the water- way by the Manchester Liners, Limited, a com- pany already noted for its enterprise, and for the magnificent vessels it owns. One of these, "The Manchester City," has become quite farnous,o wing to her vast dimensions and easy navigation of tne canal. A sister ship, owne4 by the same company, was launched at Jarrow last week with the title " Manchester Port," and a splendid example of the ship builders' skill she is. About half -a- dozen other vessels of similar class and size are in process of construction for the Manchester Liners Limited, the object of the Company being to establish a weekly service of steamers between Manchester and Canada. It is only in this way that the benefits to manufacturers accruing from the canal will be made apparent. R3gular steam- ship lines to the principal ports of the New World will do more than anything else to reduce freights, encourage transhipment of goods, and develop the great resources of the Ship Canal. LBY A CORRESPONDENT.]

The pro4pects of cricket in this district as far as can be ascertained up to the present are of anything but a rosy character and it is doubtful whether the senior club will venture on a struggle for another season. Frodsham seem to have got a lively lot of players together and should be going strongly. I understand that almost all the Runcorn players will go there for cricket this season.

The turn-out of the Runcorn Fire Brigade on Wednesday night should serve as an object lesson. The scene of the fire was a stack of hay situate in a field at the far end of Norman-road, near to the railway embankment, and it took the brigade abnut an hour mad five minutes to lay on water. In their first attempt they tried the hydrant in Cawley's- lane, but the water would hardly fill the pipe, then after other futile attempts a supply was success- fully conveyed to the seen of the conflagration. It was so puny, however, that small as the fire was a good hour was occupied in putting it out.

One doesn't want to cast reflections on the Fire Brigsle, which is composed of men to be highly re- spected, if only for the risks they undertake. But the efforts in putting out the fire were very crude. A far better result would have been obtained by using a hay-knife, and cutting off the charred out- side edge of the hay on one side, and carting off the remainder out of reach ot the flames, and binding it into trusses on the windward side. As the hay was but a small quantity, the briga-ie must be excused. I suppose, for indulging in a little hose pra?tice. My advise to householders, however, js see to get- ting out an insurance policy, and watching the brigade at practice will be a little more enjoyable.

At the Urban District Council meeting on Thurs- day morning there was a little bit of a breeze caused by the remarks of Mr. W. Collier who laid the law down as to "the duty of vice-chairmn." But Mr. Imi.;on was also in a dictatorial humour, and in a little preliminary gave the officials something upon which to reflect.Mr.lmison generally does not speak unless he has grounds tor doing so.

I am given to understand that the energetic Mis- sionary (Mr. W. Shaw) of the Mersey Mission to Seamen has secured a large field situated in the Holloway which is being fitted out as a recreation ground for sailors, so that they miy enjoy them- selves when ashore during the summer montlis.The field will be formally opened at Whitsuntide.

TRAPS IN COLLISION AT RUNCORN.

A LO3AL CASE AT THE LIVERPOOL ASSIZES'

On Saturday, befere Mr. Justice Righam and a com- mon jury, a claim was heard in which the plaintiff,elrs. Margaret Acton, such the defendant, Robert Shepherd, for damages for personal injuries alleged to have been die to the negligence of defenlant'a servant whilst driv• jug a milk flat in Aston-road, near Frodsham, Cheshire. Negligence was denied by the defendant, who pleaded nonzributory negligence on the p art of plaintiff. Two men, named Nickson, alias Barney D)rlingt .n, of Whitechapel, Weston, and Charles Stubbs, of Rock Savage, were standing near the L. and N. W. Railway Bridge just oppcmite Halton Station, when they onserved a man walking from the direction of Clifton. 0.1 arriv- ing at the bridge the man deliherately climbed the °rick work, hung over the side by his hands for a few seconds then let go acd dropped into the tix-font way beneath. —Nickson and Stubbs then went t) the man and found .him lying very much injured. They carried him to the embankment out of danger of passing trains, and subse- quently conveyed him to his borne 10, White-street,Run- corn, in a cart borrowed from Mr. Joseph Stokes, Rock Savage.—The man's name is Hodgetts,and he is now be- ing attended by Dr. Robinson for severe injuries to both legs, and at present little hope is entertained of his re- covery. The reason for the foolhardy freak is n't given, and the deliberate dropping off from a bridge about 30 feet high strongly points to an attempt at suicide.

lo THE TRICK OF A MADMAN."—PLAYING WITH PARAFFIN AT RUNCORN.

Before Mr. F. J. Nermaa' on Tuesday morninz, John Kinsey, labourer of 42, Norfolk-street, was char,ed with having on the previous day set fire ta the goods in his dwelling house, or with ;attempting to do so.—It seemed that in consequence of having had a row with his em- ployer (Mr. Orme, of Weston), his wife and he had some woris. The man, it is alleged, then went upstairs and poured some paraffin on LH , bed and sot fire to it.— Police Constable Brett gave evidence of being callei into the house. Upstairs the bed and bedding ha i been thrown in a heap and paraffin had been sprlnkled over the floor. The whole was bLizing. He s•iized the mattress and threw it downstairs and by tramping on the flames and other means extinguished the fire. The prisoner was in the house shouting,aud on being charged by the constable said he was not afraid of owning it. Prisoner had been charged that morning also in the presence of the Inspector and said " I must not have been right in my senses to do such a thing."—The Magistrate saiA it was the trick of a madman.—Prisoner said he had had a drop of drink and got into a rage;that would be a warning to him not to have any more drink. Prisoner was reminded to the next sessions. SMOKER AT THE PARISH CHURCH SCHOOLS. On Wednesday eveaing a most enjoyable smoking concert was held at the National schoolroom, Runcorn, which was largely patronised. Mr. E Imison C.C., who presided, said he was very pleased to see such a large gatlieiing of smokers. He would not be out of place by seying that the concert would be a success if it did not tend to cause boys of 14 to 18 years of age to take to the weed. It would be all right if they waited until they had matured and ,he thought himself it was soothing for a mln after a hard day's toil or business to find solace in a pipe •of good tobacco. Dr. McDougall report d the number of deaths registered during the mouth was 15, one was an inquest case. The average death rate was 10 per thousand of the ektiinated population, and exclusive of inquest cases 9.3. The number of births registered was 44. For the corresponding month of last year 33 deaths and 60 births were registered. Mr..J. Wilson moved the adoption of these minutes. Mr. Handley secoaded and it was carried. Take in water Corn &a

Mr:J. Pritchard also observed it must be a satisfaction to the Council to knew that there was a profit of £5l 19s 63 on the water undertaking, against a loss of £ll9 103 21 on the previous year. Tney had received about £2B more for water, but their expenditure hads been Very low indeed—about £ll5 less than the previoa, year. It was a satisfaction that they need not ask for the assistance of the rates at the present time. During , the five years they had been working only one penny in the had been received from the rates so that it was only occasionally they would have to ask the rates to contribute anything towards the paying of the interest and manual instalments of principal. Further if they went on as they were doing land had no exceptional expenditure, they hoped the undertaking would meet ad the dem tnds made on its re- sources. There had been some dispute about Mr. Tun- stall's reports. He had looked into the matter carefully and found that according to his reports that at least the Thuncil would regnire annually a penny in the £ from the rotes; Mr. Tarlatan reports went over considerable ground, and he had tak m the three reports together. Mr. Marshall had also gone over that matter with him and came to the fame conclusion that according to Mr. TunstalPs reports the amount that would have to be receivA out of tie rates annually was £lBl, or a trifle over a penny in the £. He thought the reason for some m,sipprehension was bhat Mr. Tanstall's reports were based upon the assump- tion that Parliament would grant a suspension of the sinking fund for at least five years; This concession was not wanted. As soon as they took over the undertaking they had to begin to pay into he sinking fund, and the value of the fund to-day was £3.183. Toe interest upon those instalments together with £566 add id every year, would make a considerable yearly amumulaion, and in a few years time the sinking fund would progress by leaps and bounds. Therefore from a financial point of view the water undertaking mlght be regarded as satis- factory. Ira addition the analysis submitted last month showed that there was ma deterioration in the quality of the water, and on the whole the und srtaking rnieht coosidered to he in a highly satisfactory condition. ME. IMISON ON THE DUTY OT OFFICIALS..

Mr. E. Imison submitted as his opinion that thP Clun- en wag gradually letting the business slip into the fluids of officials, which was not a very wise thing. He lid not think it weuld have been necessary for him to mention that again after what he had said

sometime ago but it did appear to him, owing probably to the ind,fference of the members, or to their want of attention, that a good deal of the business of the Coun- cil was done without the Coancil's kaowkd,;e. He would give au instance: Communications came to the Council which were not reponed to the Council, and answers to those communications were sent without having received the imprimatur of the Council; Tide was a distinctly bad practice:. It was bad insomuch that it might cause the councillors themselves to be regarded with a certain amount of suspicion. It was bad too be- cause it might give the officials an inflated idea of their own importance. Another irregularity that had cropped up was that reports were made to the committees perhaps not formally but informally and arrangements might be made without the knowledge of the person who made these complaints, for inquiries to be made. A committee might come to a resolu- tion, and might forward a c ommunication which had no received the sanction of the Council, and which ho w i.e told did not absolutely convey the opinions of the c mamittee upon these matters. This be- ing soil appeared to him that it would be wise for the Council to arrive at the conclusion that no communica- tioll3 should be received at the offine, addressed to the Chairman or the Council which were not reported to the Council and put on the ,agenda and that no answers should be sent to those commonicalons except sub as formally releiv:sd the sanction of the Council. ffe sup- posed he chould be out of eird:ir in moving a resolution to this effect and be felt so convinced they were getting in a loose way that he gave notice that he would move such a resolution 'lt the next Council meeting. The meeting then concluded.

3.lit. LEVI RICHARDSON ON THE" VOICE." The following extract is from "The Warrington Circuit Wesleyan Methodist Church Record " Our readers wal be glad of the opportunity et ' reading this extract from the Norwich and Ely 1 Training College Old Students' Club College Letter, of March, 1899. One morning in November, a very interesting, useful and practical lecture on " The VoiC3," was given by 'Mr. Levi Richardson, who on two occasions of paying a fling visit to Norwich, has given the College the benefit of his very wide knowledge ana experience. He expounded the principle of the matter shortly and clearly, and ptt all peent through their paces in masterly style, and, not least, he gave practical illustrations of his own precepts with his fine and cultured voice. The students were highly appreciative, and only lamented that he did not sing more than ha (lid. THE SITE FOR THE CliolaWELL STATUE. According to "The Warrington Circuit Wesleyan ' Methodist Church Record," "there is great dissatis- faction throughout the town over the site which has been cnosen for the statue. Our contemporary says: "It is a; poor and contemptible thing after having accepted a gift unwillingly, to hide it away in an inconvenient corner. The impression which prevails may be mistaken, but it looks like one of the fantastic tricks 'of vain man dressed in a little brief authority'. If the town were an Athens crowded with statues, there might be some excuse for putting Cromwell out of the way. The imagin- ation does not picture a great soul despairingly seeking amid a multitude of statues for a suitable site, hut something quite different and altogether less eclifyinz. The whole town was before whoever want forth to seek the site. It seems a pity that the proper site in front of the Town Hall should have been appropriated to a fountain. It will awaken strange thoughts in tha reflective to see a Walker fountain in front of the Town Hall, and n 'Cromwell statee at Bridge Poet. This was done at Warrington in the last year of the nineteenth cen- tury. While we feel with those who express their diesatisfaction in t?rms much stronger than we have ventured to use, we would like to point out that in one sense the site chosen is most appropri- ate, inasmuch as the '.losing scene of the Preston fight took place at Warrington Bridge. Walter Scott's Dugald Dalgetts was present at that fight. There is a graphic picture in Carlyle'e Cromwell ' of the wounded Dalg,tty. and of the scene which took place in front of the site on which Crornwell'e I alighterl ; but the con- statue is to be Placed.—' atant alarms of the enemy's approach male me re- solve to ride forward to Warrington which was but a mile ; and indeed I may say I slept all that way, notwithstanding my wound..

ItHODESIA.N SHARES.

If we Examine the London Stock 'Exchange list of quotations of training and land shares, we shall find that the shares of those companies which are devoted to the exploration and development of lands and gold mines stand at far higher prices than those of mining corn- panies proper. Among the companies carrying on this kind of work in Rhodesia, the prespects of tbe Austen (Gwolo) Development Syndicate, Limited, are second cp none, taking into consideration the fact of the smallness of its capit A, which is only £50,000, in £l. fully-paid-un shares, and that it owns a farm of 6,000 acres and 507 claims, in the test keown and most important gold mining districts of Rhodesia. The latest reports from the manager in Rhodesia state that " during the week some rich float has be 'n pi7ked no on the ttrike of the reef at the Old Dog ' Mine." " Have started to sink on that pay shoot on the Jackson, and it is paying wonderfully." "Have started some work on the Gwelo Main Reef and Clackmenan, and have a shaft going dowa on the Jacksen, where good ore was found." "Found nice specimens of visible gold from the shaft on the Jackson. Greatly ,leased with the ap- rearance of the reef ;it is reek which pans well." "Old Dog.Jessep and Jackson will mike a very nice comu4ny with four reefs and splendid site for reduction works and storage dams for water." "We have Huai good ore at Scouts in the old workings ; excellent, showing rich visible gold." The properties acquired by the Anse ten (Gwelo) Development syndicate. Limited, as nil be seen from the above, are of a magnitude which now places the company in a position to reap the great ad. vantages and profits arising from subsidiary companies formed to work portions of their large estates. At the present price of about £2 per ehare, at which the ebsree are now dealt in on the Lerden Stock Exchange, they should be a good speculative investment, as there is twerp probability ef their very shorty itnproving to many times their present price.
