OUR, LOCAL OriURCIIES.

cams rADELPHIAN MEETING ROOM.

In the Chrietadelpaian liketing RJoel, Bold-street, Mr. E. Bellamy, of Stockport, lectured on Sunday, his sub- ject being " Tneu shell the righteous shine forth as the Sun in the Kingdom of their Father." Tne Lectarer obeerved that these words of Christ brought several questions before them for consideration; What was the Kingdom? Woo were the righteous ? V4ho was tbe father of the righteous ? When would the righteous he rewarded,and what would be their reward? Paul answered all of the questions in very few words in the 8:h of Romans. Tnose who were led by the spirit cf God were sons of God and heirs; joint heirs with Christ. To those who thought heaven .eas the Kingdom of God. the Lecturer pointed out such paseages as " Thy Kingdame Come" in the Lord's prayer, which were not in accord with this idea, and Christ's " Woe unto you, scribes and pharisees, hypecrites ! " pieetided the idea of the King. dm of God being in tbe heart, for it was to "to those hypocrites that Christ said "The Kingdom of God is within yoa." The Church could not ba the King- dom of Gcd for it was an easy metter for a sick man to enter the church, and Christ represented it as almost impossible for a rich ram to enter the Kingdom. The incidents of the thief on the cross and the house of many mansions Were;very fully jealt with; popular ' ideas being compared with scriptural statements with which they alone were utterly irreconcilable. in John 20 and 17 they found recorded that Christ had not as- cended to the Father several days subsequent to the ona inwhich the thief made his request. God's house was never mentioned as. heaven at all but the temple was called God's House and was a typo of the living temple "the Church of the living God, the pillar and ground of the truth." If Christ wore going: to Heaven to prepare a place there, it would suggest the question ! Dees Heaven need any preparation? And what about others who have gone to an unprepared place. Heaven was perfect, and the Kingdom of God would be in the earth, net in heaven at all. Christ said "I will come again and receive you unto myself " and" Behold I come quickly and my reward is with me, to give every man aceerding as his word shall be." The righteous would shine forth as the sun "In the regeneration when th Son of man shall sit in the throne of his glory "and in this lete eay of the Gentiles, they had the inestimable privilege of patting themselves, in a state of relationship I to the good time coming. CROMWELL TERCENTENARY.

On Sunday morning at Cairc-szeet Chapel, the Rev. W. H. Drummond dealt with the subject of Religiou3 TOleration and Liberty of Conscience in connection with the series ol sermens on Oliver Cromwell4Af ter pointing out the way in which religioes toleration had become a living question in the 17ch century, he proceeded to make Cromivell's own belief clear by some striking quo- tations from his letters and speeches. For long he was regarded as an ambitious and unscrupulous autecrat, a schemer and charlatan in affairs of State, for whom no censure could be too bitter to be undeserved. That pre- judice ea as at last dead in the minds of all reasonable people, but there was perhaps a lingering suspicion even en the part of those who would like to think otherwise, that Cromwell'a religious policy was marked by an ale- sence/of toleration, if not by arbitrary harshness. That was an opinion founded upon prejudice and hearsay,and not upon a real study of history. On the contrary,ameng all the great statesmen of English history none bad tried to be more loyal to the principals of religious liberty and achieved a larger SUCCO39 in the face of great diflicultie Baxt,r, who was no friendly judge of the Protmor, could say of him, "Cromwell and his ;Intuit took en hem to join no religious party, but to be for the equal liberty of all." The history of English religioa for the last two hundred years bad borne upon it the impress of principles eelice the great Protector first clothed with teeny for the English mind. With much pain and struggle they bad accomplished what he tried te do,and at last the principle for which he contended, had become almost an axiom of their thought. The great statesman of modern England was also the feunder of our religious libertieo. The tolerance of Cromwell was the tolerance of a deeply religious man. Is was the cnly kind of tolerance which they need be at all anxiers to possees. There were some peple who made a great pro. tession of • Liberalism, but it was the Liberalism of indifference. They were sceptical about anything, and RO they did not care whether man felt or believed any- thing deeply. If they were intolerant of all it was better still to belie-Ye in spiritual freedom because they believed in God. Their faith in liberty must be rooted in religien. It was their timidity which made them narrow. They were afraid for truth, they were afraid for God because they saw such a Hun way into His pur- poses. Once let their minds lie open to the living influ- ences of His grace ; once let their minds he kindled into order bythe slow and sure unfolding of His truth ; once let their souls be penetrated by some feeling of the large- ness and mystery of ;God's purposes for them and for the world, and they should desire to give all men liberty to wcrship Ged according to their eorscience, and to seek for tee larger truth hich might yet break forth frem His Word; not because they doubted or denied, or held their owu convictlous lightly, tont'for this reason that religion was such a deep and real thieg to them and they had learned that it was no small part of Chris- tin piety to honour the voice of reason and conscieece, their own or another's, which was the word of God in he seul of man.

THE FORTHCOMING WYCLIFFE BAZAAR.

The Grand "Excelsior" Bazaar which is being pro- mat ,d in connection with Wycliffe Congregational Church promises to be a huge success. Arrangements are now practically completed for the opening cere- mony in Parr Hall on Tuesday afternoon, when Lieutenant-Colonel W. W. Pilkington, J.P., of Pres- cot and St. Helens will declare the bazaar open. The decorations have been entrusted to Messrs. F. Le Maistre and Co., of Manchester, and they will be on a scale of unusual beauty. Visitors will find themselves surrounded by an Alpine Village, above which the migtty mountains rear their snow capped summits in solemn grandeur And silence. In the valley—where the lowing of the cattle and the bleating ot the goats is not heard—will be found houses of every descrize ion such as may be found on the slopes of the Alps. Their quaint roofs,their corner turrets, or castellated tops, contrasting yet blending with the humble dwellings of the mouetitineers. Here is a solid stone gateway; there, a picturo-que chalet with its overhanging roofs. Here the quaint gables of the citizen's house and there the gateway of the hotel, surmounted by a clock bidding the hungry ones hurry. The mountain torrent, the heights from which the avalanches pour down their dreaded showers of snow and stories, the pine woods, the cottage of the hardy mountaineer, the appalling precipice, will all be there, and the whole scene nest vividly pour- trayed and realistically arranged. In the ante-room of the Hospice, attractions and entertainments galore will be provided. The bazaar will be brilliantly lighted by electricity supplied by the Warrington Electric Light and Power Company, Limited. After the vigorous efforts that have been put forth to attain the success of the bazaar we hope the following prediction will come true :

At the break o'dqy ht sleep profound, The TreAsurers were duly found, Still grasping like an Iron vice, A bauk-book with the strange (levies,

--BX ORLSIOR t"

The objects of the bazaar as stated in the hand- book wl.ich has been very artistically turned out by the " Examlner " Printing Works, are most worthy, and should appell ts everyone. Virecliffe Church. it nppe its, was opened for public worship in 1873, being erected upon the site of the former chapel, which had become inadequate to the re- quirements of the Church and congregation. The outlay then involved was about £9,000. 'the school buildings, with the exception of some additions mane in 1834, are of older dete than the church buildings, and have been used for day school pur- poses, there being large, mixed, and infant schools which have been in existence since 1870. The use of these premises for the work of the day school considerably unfit,s them for church purposes, and consequently the need of suitable rooms for social and religious meetings has been greatly felt for a long time. Some cottage property immediately adjoining the Church, situate in Edgeworth-street, came into the market at the beginning of the pres- ent year. As these houses had long been regarded as a deeirable and even necessary acquisition, the deacons secured the property at the auction sale for the sum of £557 103. The g-ound on which this property is situate beinz, as already state I, imme- diately adjoining the Church, it is contemplated to remove the existing coLtages and erect a building harmonious in appearance with the architecture of the Church, which shall include an assembly room capab'e of accommodating 300 persons, which would be suitable for Lectures, Week Evening Services, Congregational Meetings, &c; also a Ladies' Parlour, for the use of working and other ladies' meetings, a Vestry, &c, &c. The deign has been prepared by gMr. P. Silcock, F.R.1.8.A., of Warrington. It is believed the building can be erected for £1,700. The people ' of Wycliffe have long had the idea in their mind of providing a suitable and well-deserved memorial of their late valued and beloved friends Mr. and Mrs. Samuel Rigby, of Bruche Hall. There can be no doubt whatever that to the devoted labours, loving attachment, and large generosity of Mr. and Mrs Rigby, Congregationaliem in Warrington is incal- culably indebted. Others, of course, and some very Conspicuously, have aided the cause by faith- ful work and munificent support, but the universal feeling is that to Mr. Rigby supremely is the honour due of having rescued Congregational- ism in this town from comparative obscurity, and of having pre-eminently helped to place it upon a foundation of influence and power. For upwards of 20 years he served as deacon, for many years as treasurer, taking his share also of the work of superintending the Sunday school. His gifts to the support of the Church were mos,t. large and gener- ous. His presence could a'aveye ha reckoned upon, both Sunday and week day. " Wycliffe " always with Mr. and Mrs. Rigby, stood supreme, and no social engagements or personal gratifications were allowed to hinder their loyal and untiring service. When the time came for their removal from War- ringtod, the heaviest sorrow of the separation was that of leaving Wycliffe, and for many years after leaving Mr. Rigby continued to be a large contribu- tor to the funds. It is thought, therefore, that the new buildings now contemplated should most ap- propriately be called "The Rigby Memorial Build- ings,"hy means of which in future days not only Wycliffe people, but Warrington people of every church and party, should be reminded of a former citizen whose genial presence and generous sym- pathy were ever conspicuous and esteemed. The bazaar is intended: Ist. To discharge the debt in- curred by the purchase of the property. 2nd. To aid in providing a fund fer the erection of the memorial building. Under the direction of Mr. W. H. Payton. Mus. Bac., and Mr. R. H. Pearson an excellent orchestra will perform each even;ng in the large hall. The aide attractions will be thoroughly in keeping with the rest of the arrangements. An amusing sketch, entitled "The Bengal Tiger" will be given by a talented company of artistes; an exhibition of wax works will also be given, containing characters from history, literature, and art, and a series of tableaux, " from grave to gay, from lively to severe.' A hat trimming competition, for gentle- men only. will be held, besides other a tractions. On Wednesday, Mrs. E. Shorrock Eccles, of Huy- ton, will opsn the bazaar ;on Thursday, Mr. A. H. Crosfield, of Walton Lea, will perform the opening ceremony, and on Friday, Miss Naylor, of Cuerden Hall.

SPIRITUALISM.

The first meetings in connection with the above mission were addressed by Mr. E. Marklew on Sun- day ' • in the afternoon on "The present need." and in the evening on "The utility of Spiritualism." Both addresses wore very closely followed by the audiences. On Monday evening Mr. Marklew took questions from the audience, to which he replied, and afterwards gave a little clairvoyance. On Tut!s- day evening the meeting was addressed by Mr. James Swindlehurst, the organizing agent of the Spiritualist Federation, who dealt with the general subject of the nature and philosophy of spiritual- ism, and was followed by Mrs. Shaw, of Preston who Rave a number of clairvoyant delineations.

At the new premises of this mission, on Sunday, a musical service was held, The Ragged School was established about twelve years ago for the purpose of bringing together the poor ragged waifs and gutter children in Warrington. The children have been taught to sing and read Bible lessons and in addition to the Sunday services they have a Drum and Fife Rand which practise twice a week,a choir practioe and a girls' sewing class weekly, and there is also a good Baud of Hope in connection with the school. During the past year clothing has been supplied to some 200 children and it is needless to say the mission is doing exceedingly good work. During the service special hymns were sung,assistecl by the United Band of Hope choir, under the con- ductorship of Mr. Fanshaw. In the evening a special set vice was conducted by the Rev. F. g. Powell, vicar or S. James', Latch ford,who took for his text the words, "A certain nobleman went into a far country to receive for himself a kingdom, and to return. And he called his ten servants and said unto them, occupy, till I come" (Luke xix. 12 and 13).

WYCLIFFE SUNDAY SCHOOL ANNIVERSARY.

This event took place on Sunday and was the most successful of recent yeirs. A special effort was made to raise £5O in order that the treasurer might be relieved of any anxiety with reference to the school funds. About 600 schclars are on the school registers and they are under ihe superintendence of Mr. George Wallington. the Rev. Professor Armitage, M.A., of Yorkshire United College, Bradford, was the preacher for the occasion and his scholarly discourses were greatly appreciated by the large congregations which assembled. Within the com- munion rails a choice display of flowers and plants had been tastefully arranged by Mrs. Z. Armitage and Mrs. Pearson. At the morning service, the children were accommodated in the galleries, and they sweetly sang a number of hymns. The choir very effectively rendered the anthem "We praise Thee, 0 Lord," the solo part be- ing taken by Mr Goldthorpe whose bass voice was heard to great advantage. The preacher based his dis- course en the text" For we are labourers to.-ether with God ;ye are Goi's husbandry, ye are God's building," I Corinthians. 33, 9v. Be remarked that these were the key words of life and pointed out that in them lay the secret of strength and the secret of rest. In desc;ibing the posi ion of man:he showed his alliance with the great forces of nature which marked his superiority over the lower animals. _ _

In the afternron at three o'clock an open meeting of the P.S.A. washld in the church when special Sunday school hymns were sung by the scholars and friends. The church was completely filled. Mrs. Robert Garnett of Penk th presided • In the course of her address she said one ef;the glories of England was its Sunday schoosl Inroads were being made on the Sabbath day and it was their duty to resist them. She did not speak from a re- ligious point of view but from a physical point of view• They must have a day of rest.and if they allowed it tojhe filched away from them under the pretence of more recreation they would find that they would get more work t) do and that they would be losers instead of gainers. She knew something of what the Continental Sunday meant. In France many of the mills were work- ing on Sunday and the operatives were employed 10 hours a day. They did not want that sort of thing in England, and they most not let Sunday drift into the stream with the other days of the week. She was glad there were meetings for men in that church every Sun- day. The speaker proceeded to refer to her deep symeathy with Sunday school teachers. A very great responsibility rested upon them. They must work on in faith arid trust, always hoping that the labour is not lost. She hoped that anniversary day wculd be a very b:essed one, and that they might have very haepy re- collections of it ard pit.aaing results. (Applause.) Mrs .le.S. ARMITAGE, in the course of an exeellent add- ress said that they were very near the beginning of the 20th century. There was semething sonemn about the be- ginning of a new year, and there should he someth'ng more solemn inlthe beginif ng of anew century. What the neat century would be depended upon young people like many of those present. It would be what they made it, and if they made their character whilst they were youag they would help to make the c^ntury bqter than any that had preceded it. It was a very difficult thing to re-make a character at the age of 30 or 40 years. She felt sure that the next century would be the reign of the. people. That reign had begun now and they might he quite sure that it would develop more and mar e Things were very different already te what they were 50 years ago, when workingmen had no views whatever in the Government of the country. If the worSing men used their power, they would to supreme in the Govern- ment. Some people were lesing faith in democracy. It was because they did not believe in the forces that were , renewing and transforming character. The success of democracy depended upon the character of the people. : God had given to us the greatest empire the world had ever seen, and if we acknowledged it in the right way we phould take up the burden and fulfil our data, to- wards it. God gave us that empire for some great pur- pose. He intended that we should use it to ble33 the other nations of the earth, and if we did rot use itaight, Ithe csnJielccneu:.l be taken from us and some other would be air, in irs place. Eagland was t:•ying her best to bring about a righteous Government and to do justly. How was it that England gave justice without bribes ? If Englishmed were to f,il 'n character and cease to be just, then the glory of England would disappear, rand she was sure the Empire would go toe. How were they to keep up the National charact r ? That was the burning question Itoday. It was more important than social reform. 'I he question f supreme importance was : Hew is character Ito be kept up ? They who were Christians beieved they ' had, the answer to that question. They believed id Jesue Christ. They gathered children in Sunday schocla to learn of Him and enter whilst children into,livisg re-

lationship with God. Religion was a relationship. 10 was not a matter of teaching certain truths and propok" tions that could be committed to memory and learned by heart. They must make Jesus, Bing. If our countrt held the springs of character in Christ,our country woule keep her high place amongst the nations. It would not' have to mourn that she bad fallen from bet high estate. There was no greater wort than trying to touch the springs or character bi bringing young people into relationship with Christ. The speaker proceed( d to speak words of eocouragerneDo te teachers and parents. In conclusion she said abet had lately celebrated the tercentenary of the birth eg Oliver Cromwell, that great and typical Englishman. Young people ought to know who laid the foundatiove of civil and religious liberty in England. There was book published a short time ago in France,which create' a great sensation, entitled " What is the reason of the' superiority of the Anglo-Saxon ?" The writer said that Englishmen were trained to depend upon theraselves,sott Frenchmen to look to the State. The writer might ha/ added that the English peeple had had a religion, Oa espenially the:Englishmen of the Free Churches, whole religion had taught him that he was responsible direct!! to God. Many people said their Coogreeational Church were played out, and that they should give up what the! had feueht fcr and go over to the Estatlished Church. How could they do so when the priest was saying "Cor fide your charactcr to me, I will guide you into all the!. is necessary. I am the m4ker of the blessed sacrament, i• am the shadow of spiritual grace and power ?" There never was a time when Congregational Churches sycire more needed than at the present. Might they chernib that neble idea" that the Church lives to serve." (er plause.) Mr. G. WALLINGTON thanked Mrs. Garnett.", and BM. Armitage for their addresses,and others who had assistew in the service ; also Mrs. Armitage and Mrs. Pearson for decorating the front of the pulpit with flowers. CHANCELLOR ESPIN AT WARRINGTON. THE CRISIS IN THE CHURCir.

Chancellor Eenin held a Court at the Parish Church, Warrington, on Thuredty Ist week for the admission of wardens and eilesmen. Prior to delivering his c arge, morning prayer was said, the service being conducted by the Rector (Rev. Canon Willis) assisted by the Revs. A. F. White and Cameron. The CHANCELLOR, in delivering kis charge, said be thought there were no churches in that neighbourhood in which the wardens still retained church rates. They depended almost entirely upon voluntary offerings of some kind or other. For his part he could not but think that the abolition of church rates was in some re- spects to be regretted. When church (xp ruses were pro- vided out of rates in the old days and the rate laid in the vestry there was an opportunity for the parishioners to express their views as to the conduct of the services and so on. Now, if a clergyman was a man of means or if he had a wealthy parishiener to support him he might practically do what he liked within the broad limits of the law. These remarks, he felt, did not apply to Warrington and district Decease he believed the ser- vices of the church were conducted in nearly every case, to the satisfaction of the parishioners who did not seem to be backward in supplying the wherewithal for the maintenance of the services. He did not think volun- tary contributions would be forthcoming from free Englishmen in the way they were for the maintenance of services of which these Englishmen did not approve. The Bishop .suggested,and he entirely agree d with him, that the churchwarnens should be re- minded at that time of the extreme importance of their regular attendance at church. The B shop also con- sidered that it would be well if the churchwardens noted and observed, and, if necessary, reported, any omission of any part of the Prayer Book services. He did not know that such OMiSEiOI:I3 occurrei at all in that diocese. The layman had the right of the full services of the Prayer Book as they were printed. They ought to be rendered to him, and he had a right to demand that they should be so rendered without any omissions. A churchwarden. however, could not interfere directly and personally with the officiating minister. If they saw anything which was conceived to b irregular Cr anything omitted or anything that appeared to be an illegal innovation they were quite ju:trfieci in calling his attention privately to it awl if that did not secure redress the churchwardens could take further steps and report it to the bishop. It was highly improper and very wrong for churchwardens or sides nen to interfere in the conduct of Divine service. On more than one occasion churehwardens had suffered by taking too much upon themselves by interfering in this direction. He thought he must draw the attention of those present to the tact that the Diocesan Finance Association did not secure that universal support to which it was entitl(d. There were parishes—and some too in that district —which had received reid from one or other of the Diocsean societies and yet did not give anyting in the way of contributions either by voluntary offertories or subscriptions. The Chancellor proceeded to refer to these diocesan institutions at some length. One of them, the Board of Education, deserved the most strenuous and most liberal help church people could give it. Oce was expected, he supposed, to make some mention cf what was called the crisis in the church.lie believed they were all looking on in that centre at the crisis—if crisis there be—and were very little disturbed in their own parishes by it. In thet respect they were to be envied. For his own part he could not deny, and did not wish to deny, that there were clergymen who did indulge in extrava- gances of ritual which were quite contr try to the spirit of the prayer book and the Spirit of the English Refor- mation. He thought if they would be a little patient and leave it to the Archbishops and Bishops they would find that these extravagances would be abated. He did not himself believe there was any such thing as a general alienation of the laity from the clergy or any such thing as general dissatisfaction with the render:mg of the ser- vices of the Church. He noticed as he went round every three years that there was a very considerable augmen- tation to the maintenance of Church services. In con- clusion the Chancellor thanked the outgoing church- wardens for their services and expressed the hope that the new officers would emulate their predecessors in do- ing their duty with thoroughness.

LOCAL WEDDING.

BARBER—FLEMING. Charming weather greeted the occasion of the marriage solemnized at St. Marie's Catholic Church, Sheffield, last Thursday, between Miss Valeria Beatrice Fleming, second daughter of the late Coun- cillor J. H. Fleming' J.P , of Springfield-streett Warrington, and Dr. Barker Barber, of Glossop- road, Sheffield, son of the late Dr. Barber, of that city. The marriage took place from Leighwood, Pittsmore, and the ceremony wa..2 performed by the Very Rev. Dean Dolan,of Sheffield .The bride,who was given away by her brother, Mr. John Herbert Flem- ing, was married in her travelling dress of blue pop- lin embroidered with steel torquoise, trimmed with white brocaded satin and white sashes, and wore a grey hat with white feathers and white roses. She carried a bouquet of choic2st white flowers. The bridesmaids were ; Miss Tessie Fleming and Miss Nora Fleming (sisters of the bride), and they were most tastefully attired in grey silk dresses trimmed with lace and white muslin fichu, and wore large picture hats trimmed with heliotrope feathers. The best man was Mr. David Davy, of Sheffield. The relations were afterwards entertained to breakfast at the bridegroom's house,Glossop-road. There were a large number of expensive presents which in : Bride to bridegroom, diamond pin; and bridegroom to bride,diamond and sapphire bracelet. The happy couple later in the day left for London and the South.

THE FREE CIIURCH COUNCIL AND

SUNDAY NEWSPAPERS.

At a meeting of the Executive of the Free Church Council held in Bold:street Schoolroom on Friday afternoon the following resolution was passed : "That this committee of the Warrington and District Evangelical Free Churches is convinced that the issue of Sunday editions of the "Daily Telegraph " and of the "Daily Mail " is fraught with incalculable mischief to the industry, moral character and strength of our countrymen. It will rob the journalist and printer of his •Satur- day's rest, and ultimately extract from them seven days' work for six day-.' pay; it. will burden thousands of over-worked newsagents and distribu- tors of newspapers with another day's totl ; it will create' a revolution in our industrial life by adding another work day to , the week, and so re- inforce those conditions in modern life which lower its tone, deba‘e its ideals, and destroy its usefulness. Therefore this Committee advises all Free Church, men and Free Churchwomen to free themselves from the slightest complicity with this attack on that most precious British heritage, viz : our right to cessation from toil on one day in seven, by (1) Refusing to purchase a copy of these papers in the week as long as the Sunday 68110 is con- tinued. (2) Refusing to advertise in the columns of any daily paper that has a"Stutday edition ; and, (3) Supporting news-agents who refuse to sup- ply Sunday newspapers."

CYCLING IN FRANCE.

Sir.—As considerable misapprehension appears to ex- ist in the minds of the general public as to the forrnali• ties which have to be observed by cvci.sts visiting France, I shall be glad if you will permit m^ to explain that the payments required of the tourist whose stay does not extend over a period of three months are : (1) The Customs duty of 220 francs per 100 kilo- grammes or. roughly, 30s per machine. (2) A stamp duty ot 20 centime., charged upon the temporary permit which takes the place of the metal plaque every resident whe Amen receives, and has to produce as evidence that he has paid the cycle tax of 6 francs. The amount depo;ited under (1) is refunded—but usually only after nairh vexatious delay—when the cycle leaves the c nintry; tout the member of the National Cycling Association—such as the Cyclirts' Touring Club —officially recognised oy the Fr-gocti Goverement, is free to enter without r ayment upon production of the necessary credentials, _

The stamp duty ot 60 centimes chameable under (2) bas to be p Üby all visiting cyclists without distinc- tion, and it is, of course, not r4turnable. It will be col- lected on and from to-thy by the Customs when the machine is lanthd, but the C.T.C. has already made arrangements whereby the &lay that usually attaches to formalities of this description will, at any rate in ths case of its members, be reduced to the lowest limit. George Henry Hassln, of 140, Graftun-street, St. Helens, a labourer employed in condection wi:h :hi sul- phate plant at Messrs. Pilkington's sheet .glass works, had a nArraw escape from a horrible death on Sunday morning. Hans3n, while going about his work, had cccasion to cross over a large tank, which was partly filled with boiling gas water from the gas-producers, when he overstepped or slipped off a plank iiro the tank. DEDICA.TION OF aitgAlo.t[LlL WINDJWS .AT NEWTON.

There was a large and fashionable gataering at St. Peter's Caurch, Newton on Saturday afternoon in con- nection with the dedication of four stained glass me- morial windows. Arnoegst those present were L er.l Newton, Co:olel Richard Pilkingtoe, M.P., Mr r J Gil- lespie, J.P., Mr G McCorcieedaie, Mr A. L and Mrs McCorquodele, Mr H and Mn Dudgeon, Mrs AcC )rquo. dale, Mrs Greeashields,Dr Watkins,and otta-rs Me prin- cipal window which occupied the East end of the church was erected to the memory ot the late Colonel Meer rquo- dale, whose many geaeroas acts in connection wit n the church are of a uoteworthy character, and it is due to his family that the old chu:ci has been demelisho and the present handsome s,ructure erected in its place. Toe window is the outcome of public suoscription, and represents an outlay of about £6OO the suoj,ect being "The Ascension." On the extreme north near the organ is another window erected by Mrs McCorquodale to the nismery of her late huibami, and represents the prophets "Isaiah, ieramian. and Ez Ariel." The other windew is above the chaecel and is a representative. of "John da Wycliffe and R obert Corostete," to the memory of George A.r:nitage Smith by his nephew. Toe usual service for evening prayer, was held, the lessons beine road by the Revs. T. ft. Matthews and Smith of Warrington. Toe dedieation shin d nave been performed by the Rev. Archdeacon Maiden, but as he was unable to be present, the ceremony was performed by the Rev Canon Penrhya, rector of Winwick. At the close of the service the Rev gentleman delivered an in- teresting discourse from the words " Thes is none other than the house of God; this is the gate of Heaven." He said it was only at very short notice in the midst of the pressure of much other work that he found himself called upon, in the regietted but unavoidable absence of the Archdeacon of Warrington to take part teat day in that service. The service was a service for the dedica- tion of several wind owe, and when he spoke of the de- dication of windows he meant the giving up of them to God, anti there were three points on which he should like that afternoon to say a very few words to them. First in regard to the fact on which the windows were dedicated to the glory or God and the adornment of that house; secondly, they were memorials to those wno had done good work in the parish and had gone to their rest; and thirdly it was the uniting of many together in carrying out these good works. Almost all of them knew well what a contrast there was between the church they were now in, and that whiehyprecedei it. r hey recol- lected tint the old church was one which was re- garded mean and unworthy, one in which they could take no good pride, one of which they migat well per- haps be ashamed. Now they had that beautiful spacious edifice in which they met together, one in which they might all well rejoice and be thankful, particularly to that Vicar through whom in a great degreii that build- ing was erected, and they might well think th tt it was a privilege for them to be able to take part in the adorn- mmt of the House of God. They might rejoice in think- ing that God would accept that offering for the adorn- ment of His houseeind,they must remenaner that whether it be more or less beautiful the church was God's Weise. That was the great thing for them to remmober. The windows were memorials and tokens of family affection and recollection of those who ht . gone, and they were also reminders to Mica' of those who had gone before, and what they were, and of what they had done and of the good works they had carried out. When they saw windows put in in that way to the glory of God and to the memory of this and that person they recalled to them those people, and should prove to theta incentives to follow after the good works of those who hid gone before. They should strive to carry on such good works in service to the Lord. The windows wcre not, he believed, the gift of one single individual or of one family,butthe united work of the parishioners in general, many having joined together in this good work. In the evening an organ rectal was given by Mr. T. Sharpies, Miss. Bac. F.R.C,0., organist of Christ Church Salford, interspersed with vocal selections. Tears was a large congregation, and the following programme was excellently submitted :—Organ, "Sonata in F minor" (Mendelssohn) ; solo, Miss G. Brewnhill ; organ, "Meditation,",' Bell Rondo" ; solo, "There is a green hill far away," Mr. Hindley Smith ; organ," Andante in G," "Toccata in G" ; solo, Miss L. LIFE PEERS.

At the 11th annual meeting of the Lancashire and. Cheshire Conservative Workingmen's Federa- tion. held at Rawtenstall on Saturiay, Cuncillor A. Bennett (Warrington) moved, "Filar, in the opinion of this conference, the time is opportune for strengthening the constitution of the House of Lords by the introduction of the Colonial and other life peers." He thought it would be a great advan- tage to have the Colonies represented in the House of Lords,so that they could profit by the advic and assistance of the Colonial representatives in times of crisis and difficulty. GIRL'S CLUB, SILVER-STREET, WARRINGTON. 01 Weduesday. May 3ri, tie chsing meeting of this club was held (sad after a substantial tea,kindly provided by .Mr. W. Silcozk, a very pleasant even. ing was spent in music, song, and recitations. The past season lt.s been marked by great progress and improvement amongst the girls, and in every way most satisfactory results have been obtained and egpecial acknowleigment of this was made by Mrs Garnett who presidel and referred to Mrs. Davies, the Matron's, most painstaking efforts. Au interesting feature of the programme WllB the presentation of neck shawls to tl all those who had made 100 out of 104 possible attendances. These were also the gifts of the same kind donor. Another gratifying item was the dis- tribution of stockings knitted by the girls them- selves with wool supplied by the ladies. Amongst those pre.ient were Miss Dutton,Miss G. Sloane,Miss tsanc, Miss Davey, the Misses Collin, Miss Holt, and Miss As!idown, the secretary. Thanks are due to all w.:o have helped by gilts or personal attendance in sustaining this useful though unostentatious institution through the past winter and it would he well if such beneficent missions were multiplied in our town.
